SECTION 17: CONCLUSION & NEXT STEPS
17.1 Summary
The Commercial Makeup Artist role represents a dynamic and demanding creative profession at the heart of the fashion and beauty industry's commercial visual culture. Success in this role demands:


1. Technical Excellence: Mastery of makeup application and techniques
2. Commercial Focus: Understanding brand objectives and commercial outcomes
3. Speed & Efficiency: Ability to work quickly and consistently
4. Business Acumen: Marketing, pricing, client management
5. Collaboration: Effective teamwork with photographers, stylists, and models
6. Resilience: Ability to handle pressure and competition
7. Adaptability: Flexibility to changing trends and client needs
8. Professionalism: Reliability, punctuality, and ethical conduct


According to industry research, the most successful commercial makeup artists are those who have developed a distinctive aesthetic while maintaining the business acumen to navigate the competitive landscape. The role is evolving rapidly with the adoption of digital technologies, with AI tools, virtual try-on technologies, and the growing demand for inclusive beauty representation.


Recent industry research reveals significant challenges facing fashion creatives, including makeup artists: only 14% are paid on time, 79% have felt pressured to work for free, and 83% report tolerating toxic workplace behaviors. The future belongs to commercial makeup artists who can combine creative vision, technical mastery, and business acumen while advocating for fair treatment and professional standards. The commercial makeup artistry industry is projected to continue growing, driven by increasing demand for beauty content across advertising and digital platforms.

EOEX FASHION INDUSTRY JOB PROFILES COMPENDIUM
Document 55 of 60+: HAIR STYLIST - COMMERCIAL (Coiffeur / Coiffeuse Commercial / Hair Stylist Publicitaire / Beauty Hair Artist)


The Elegance of Excellence - Complete Career Framework


________________


EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the Commercial Hair Stylist role within the fashion and beauty industries, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to senior leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The Commercial Hair Stylist is an essential creative professional at the heart of fashion and beauty production, responsible for translating commercial visions into reality through the artistry of hair design. According to the National Careers Service, hair stylists "advise clients on haircare and hairstyles, shampoo and condition hair, cut, colour and style hair, sometimes for fashion shows, film, TV and theatre productions." In the commercial context, this role is distinguished by its focus on brand objectives, commercial viability, and mass-market appeal, requiring a unique combination of technical expertise, commercial awareness, speed, and consistency.


The profession has become increasingly complex and demanding in recent years. A landmark 2024 survey by Fashion UK (Bectu) of over 500 fashion creatives revealed significant challenges facing all fashion professionals, including hair stylists: "just one in ten fashion creatives (10%) say they feel secure in their job" and "only 14% are paid on time for the work they do." A staggering "eight in 10 (79%) have felt pressured to work for free," while "83% say that behaviours that would be considered toxic and inappropriate in public life are often tolerated in the fashion industry." The survey also found that "69% of respondents are not paid for overtime, with 24% working 11-hour days or more" and "51% earn less than £30,000 a year." Nearly a third of respondents (31%) "don't see themselves in the industry in five years' time."


The commercial hair styling profession spans diverse specializations, from advertising campaigns and e-commerce shoots to catalog productions, brand activations, and retail experiences. The profession requires a unique combination of technical expertise, creative vision, and exceptional collaboration skills. Hair stylists must master diverse techniques including cutting, colouring, styling, and wig work, while staying current with rapidly evolving trends. As noted by industry experts, "the ability to interpret a creative brief accurately and efficiently is just as important as technical skill." The profession also demands significant business acumen, as many hair stylists work freelance, requiring them to manage their own finances, marketing, and client relationships.


This document provides a detailed gender-based analysis of commercial hair styling requirements, comprehensive career pathways, value chain analysis, command chain mapping, key stakeholder identification, red flags, pain points, and vectors for long-term success. It also includes profiles for each major commercial hair styling sub-category, from advertising and e-commerce to retail and brand activations.


________________


SECTION 1: CLASSIFICATION & IDENTIFICATION
1.1 Official Classification
Attribute
	Detail
	French Job Title
	Coiffeur / Coiffeuse Commercial / Hair Stylist Publicitaire / Beauty Hair Artist / Coiffeur de Studio
	English Job Title
	Commercial Hair Stylist / Advertising Hair Stylist / Beauty Hair Artist / Studio Hair Stylist
	ROME Code
	D1205 - Coiffure; L1502 - Costume et habillage spectacle
	Convention Collective
	Coiffure - IDCC 2856; Presse Spécialisée Mode - IDCC 303; Spectacle - IDCC 3090
	Job Family
	Creative Production / Beauty & Fashion / Commercial

	Supervises
	Hair Assistants, Junior Hair Stylists, Interns
	Location
	Paris / London / Milan / New York / Los Angeles / Sydney / Tokyo
	1.2 Professional Context
The Commercial Hair Stylist operates across multiple creative domains, each with distinct requirements and expectations:


Advertising Campaign Hair Styling: Artists working on advertising campaigns, beauty campaigns, and commercial fashion features. This work involves creating looks for high-production campaigns, often requiring conceptual and commercial approaches that push creative boundaries while maintaining brand alignment. According to job descriptions, commercial hair stylists must demonstrate "the ability to interpret a commercial brief accurately and efficiently," which is "as important as technical skill."


E-commerce & Studio Hair Styling: Artists working on high-volume product photography and video shoots for online retail. This requires exceptional speed, consistency, and the ability to create looks that photograph and video well across multiple angles and lighting conditions. The focus is on commercial viability and efficiency.


Retail & Salon Hair Styling: Many commercial hair stylists maintain salon work as a foundation for their commercial career, providing steady income while building their commercial portfolio. According to industry research, some stylists maintain salon positions or part-time roles to support their commercial work. This dual career path allows stylists to develop their skills while maintaining financial stability.


Brand Activation & Event Hair Styling: Artists working on brand activations, experiential marketing events, and consumer-facing experiences. This requires exceptional interpersonal skills, the ability to work quickly, and the capacity to represent the brand's image effectively.


Catalog & Lookbook Hair Styling: Artists working on seasonal catalogs and lookbook productions. This requires consistency, efficiency, and the ability to create cohesive looks across multiple garments and styles.


Social Media & Digital Content Hair Styling: Artists working on social-first content, UGC-style content, and digital campaigns. This requires platform-native aesthetics, speed, and the ability to create content that resonates with digital audiences.

	2.4 Alternative Entry Pathways
The commercial hair styling profession allows for alternative entry pathways. According to industry professionals, many successful commercial hair stylists:


* Start in Salons: Build foundational skills in commercial salons
* Assist Established Stylists: Learn on the job while building portfolio
* Attend Commercial Shoots: Network and gain experience
* Build Portfolio through Test Shoots: Create commercial-quality images
* Self-Teaching: Learn through practice, observation, and continuous learning

SECTION 4: HR JOB DESCRIPTION
4.1 Position Overview (Commercial/Advertising Hair Stylist)
Position Title: Commercial Hair Stylist (Coiffeur / Coiffeuse Commercial)


Department: Creative / Production / Commercial


Reports To: Creative Director / Art Director / Head of Hair / Production Manager


Location: Paris, France (with potential travel to production sites)


Employment Type: Freelance / Full-time / Agency-represented
4.2 Purpose of the Role
To create compelling, commercially viable hair styling that communicates brand identity, showcases products, and drives consumer engagement and sales. According to industry descriptions, the Commercial Hair Stylist combines technical expertise, artistic vision, and commercial awareness to deliver high-quality hair styling for advertising, retail, and digital platforms. The role is distinguished by its focus on commercial outcomes, requiring stylists to balance creative vision with brand objectives and commercial viability.

SECTION 17: CONCLUSION & NEXT STEPS
17.1 Summary
The Commercial Hair Stylist role represents a dynamic and demanding creative profession at the heart of the fashion and beauty industry's commercial visual culture. Success in this role demands:


1. Technical Excellence: Mastery of cutting, colouring, and styling
2. Commercial Focus: Understanding brand objectives and commercial outcomes
3. Speed & Efficiency: Ability to work quickly and consistently
4. Business Acumen: Marketing, pricing, client management
5. Collaboration: Effective teamwork with photographers, stylists, and models
6. Resilience: Ability to handle pressure and competition
7. Adaptability: Flexibility to changing trends and client needs
8. Professionalism: Reliability, punctuality, and ethical conduct


According to industry research, the most successful commercial hair stylists are those who have developed a distinctive aesthetic while maintaining the business acumen to navigate the competitive landscape. The role is evolving rapidly with the adoption of digital technologies, with AI tools, virtual try-on technologies, and the growing demand for inclusive beauty representation.


Recent industry research reveals significant challenges facing fashion creatives, including hair stylists: only 14% are paid on time, 79% have felt pressured to work for free, and 83% report tolerating toxic workplace behaviors. The future belongs to commercial hair stylists who can combine creative vision, technical mastery, and business acumen while advocating for fair treatment and professional standards. The commercial hair styling industry is projected to continue growing, driven by increasing demand for beauty content across advertising and digital platforms.

EOEX FASHION INDUSTRY JOB PROFILES COMPENDIUM
Document 56 of 60+: PRODUCTION MANAGER - COMMERCIAL (Responsable de Production Commerciale / Directeur de Production Publicitaire / Producer Mode)


The Elegance of Excellence - Complete Career Framework


________________


EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the Commercial Production Manager role within the fashion industry, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to senior leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The Commercial Production Manager represents the operational engine of the fashion commercial production ecosystem, responsible for planning, organizing, and supervising the end-to-end production of advertising campaigns, e-commerce shoots, catalog productions, lookbooks, and brand activations. According to industry definitions, the Production Manager "plans and organizes the production of commercial content and makes the necessary adjustments according to the evolution of orders, taking into account QHSE and traceability constraints." This role is pivotal in the commercial fashion industry, overseeing the entire production cycle, from concept development to final delivery, ensuring alignment with critical path timelines and margin targets.


The role demands a unique combination of technical expertise, strategic planning, and strong leadership skills, requiring the ability to negotiate with vendors, manage complex production logistics, and lead diverse teams. According to industry descriptions, the Production Manager "organizes, optimizes, and supervises means and manufacturing processes, with the objective of producing commercial content according to quality, cost, deadline, quantity, safety, and environmental imperatives." The role "applies the industrial policy defined by management" and "can supervise a team or service and manage its budget."


The profession has become increasingly complex and demanding in recent years. According to industry research, production delays, cost pressure, and sustainability compliance are among the top challenges facing fashion production executives. The Production Manager must navigate global supply chains, manage vendor relationships across multiple geographies, and integrate sustainability criteria into the production function. This requires a unique combination of technical expertise, strategic vision, and strong leadership skills.


This document provides a detailed gender-based analysis of commercial production management requirements, comprehensive career pathways, value chain analysis, command chain mapping, key stakeholder identification, red flags, pain points, and vectors for long-term success. It also includes profiles for each major production sub-category, from advertising campaign production to e-commerce, catalog, and brand activation production.


________________


SECTION 1: CLASSIFICATION & IDENTIFICATION
1.1 Official Classification
Attribute
	Detail
	French Job Title
	Responsable de Production Commerciale / Directeur de Production Publicitaire / Producer Mode / Chef de Projet Production
	English Job Title
	Commercial Production Manager / Advertising Production Manager / Fashion Producer / Production Director
	ROME Code

	Convention Collective
	Couture Parisienne - IDCC 303 (Classification Niveau I-II); Audiovisuel - IDCC 3043
	Job Family

	Reports To
	Executive Producer / Head of Production / Operations Director / COO
	Supervises
	Production Coordinators, Production Assistants, Studio Managers, Shoot Producers, Logistics Staff
	Location
	Paris / Milan / London / New York / Los Angeles / Shanghai / Tokyo
	1.2 Professional Context
The Commercial Production Manager operates at the heart of the fashion commercial production value chain, serving as the critical link between creative vision, production execution, and commercial delivery. According to industry descriptions, the role encompasses several key dimensions:


* Strategic Planning: Applying the production strategy defined by management and translating it into operational objectives
* Operational Execution: Overseeing the entire production process from concept to final delivery
* Team Leadership: Managing and mentoring production teams, often across multiple shoots and locations
* Financial Management: Managing production budgets, negotiating costs, and ensuring margin targets are met
* Quality Assurance: Maintaining the highest quality standards and ensuring commercial compliance
* Vendor Management: Managing relationships with studios, equipment suppliers, and production partners
* Continuous Improvement: Driving process optimization and efficiency improvements
* Sustainability: Integrating sustainable practices into commercial production


According to industry job descriptions, the Production Manager oversees "the end-to-end execution of the commercial production cycle, ensuring timely delivery, exceptional quality, and adherence to budget targets" while playing "a pivotal role in driving operational excellence, vendor collaboration, and continuous process improvement."


The profession spans multiple specializations:


Specialization
	Focus
	Key Requirements
	Advertising Campaign Production
	High-production campaigns, TV, print, and digital
	Creative production expertise, client management
	E-commerce Production
	High-volume product photography and video
	Speed, efficiency, volume management
	Catalog Production
	Seasonal catalogs, product showcases
	Consistency, efficiency, scale management
	Lookbook Production
	Seasonal collections, brand lookbooks
	Brand alignment, seasonal coordination
	Brand Activation Production
	Events, experiential marketing
	Event management, logistics expertise
	Studio Production
	In-house studio operations
	Studio management, workflow optimization
	Post-Production
	Editing, retouching, color grading
	Post-production management, quality control

SECTION 4: HR JOB DESCRIPTION
4.1 Position Overview
Position Title: Commercial Production Manager (Responsable de Production Commerciale)


Department: Production / Operations / Commercial


Reports To: Executive Producer / Head of Production / Operations Director


Location: Paris, France (with international travel to production sites)


Employment Type: Full-time, Senior Management
4.2 Purpose of the Role
According to industry descriptions, the Production Manager "organizes, optimizes, and supervises means and manufacturing processes, with the objective of producing commercial content according to quality, cost, deadline, quantity, safety, and environmental imperatives." The role "applies the industrial policy defined by management" and "can supervise a team or service and manage its budget."


In the commercial fashion context, the role oversees "the end-to-end execution of the commercial production cycle, ensuring timely delivery, exceptional quality, and adherence to budget targets" while playing "a pivotal role in driving operational excellence, vendor collaboration, and continuous process improvement."
4.3 Key Responsibilities

	Frequency

	Develop and lead production schedules, ensure timely resource allocation
	Seasonal
	Production Execution
	Allocate production across studios and vendors to meet delivery timelines
	Seasonal
	Budget Management
	Negotiate vendor costs; track and manage production-related spending
	Ongoing
	Vendor Management
	Build and maintain strong relationships with studios, equipment suppliers
	Ongoing
	Quality Control
	Oversee quality control processes; proactively manage quality issues
	Ongoing
	Team Leadership
	Manage and mentor production team; delegate tasks effectively
	Ongoing

	Oversee creation of production timelines, manage shoot schedules
	Ongoing

	Ongoing

	Ongoing

	Drive continuous improvement initiatives
	Ongoing

	Negotiate vendor costs and production agreements to optimize margins

SECTION 17: CONCLUSION & NEXT STEPS
17.1 Summary
The Commercial Production Manager role represents a strategic profession in the fashion industry, requiring a unique combination of technical expertise, strategic planning, and leadership skills. Success in this role demands:


1. Production Planning: Ability to develop and execute production plans
2. Technical Excellence: Deep understanding of commercial production processes
3. Leadership: Managing and mentoring production teams
4. Financial Acumen: Understanding of costs, margins, and budget management
5. Vendor Management: Building and maintaining strong vendor relationships
6. Quality Management: Ensuring consistent quality standards
7. Process Improvement: Driving continuous improvement
8. Risk Management: Identifying and mitigating production risks


According to industry research, the most successful commercial production managers are those who combine technical expertise with strong leadership and financial acumen. The role is evolving with the adoption of digital technologies, including AI-powered production management tools and automated quality control systems. The rise of sustainable production practices has also transformed the role, with production managers increasingly responsible for integrating circular economy principles and reducing environmental impact.


The commercial production industry is projected to continue growing, driven by increasing demand for commercial content across advertising and digital platforms. The future belongs to production managers who can combine operational excellence with strategic vision and sustainable practices.

EOEX FASHION INDUSTRY JOB PROFILES COMPENDIUM
Document 57 of 60+: POST-PRODUCTION MANAGER (Responsable Post-Production / Directeur de Post-Production / Chef de Post-Production)


The Elegance of Excellence - Complete Career Framework


________________


EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the Post-Production Manager role within the fashion industry, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to senior leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The Post-Production Manager represents the operational and creative engine of the post-production ecosystem, responsible for planning, organizing, and supervising the end-to-end post-production workflow for commercial fashion content, including image retouching, video editing, color grading, sound design, and final asset delivery. According to industry definitions, the Post-Production Manager "plans and organizes post-production activities and makes the necessary adjustments according to the evolution of project requirements, taking into account quality, budget, and timeline constraints." This role is pivotal in the commercial fashion industry, overseeing the entire post-production cycle, from raw asset ingestion to final delivery, ensuring alignment with creative vision, brand standards, and commercial objectives.


The role demands a unique combination of technical expertise, creative understanding, strategic planning, and strong leadership skills, requiring the ability to manage complex post-production workflows, negotiate with external vendors, and lead diverse teams of editors, retouchers, colorists, and sound designers. According to industry descriptions, the Post-Production Manager "organizes, optimizes, and supervises post-production processes, with the objective of delivering commercial content according to quality, cost, deadline, and brand imperatives." The role "applies the post-production strategy defined by management" and "can supervise a team or service and manage its budget."


The profession has become increasingly complex and demanding in recent years. According to industry research, post-production delays, cost pressure, and technological change are among the top challenges facing fashion post-production executives. The Post-Production Manager must navigate rapidly evolving technologies, manage remote and international post-production teams, and integrate AI-powered tools into the workflow. This requires a unique combination of technical expertise, creative vision, and strong leadership skills.


This document provides a detailed analysis of post-production management requirements, comprehensive career pathways, value chain analysis, command chain mapping, key stakeholder identification, red flags, pain points, and vectors for long-term success. It also includes profiles for each major post-production sub-category, from image retouching and video editing to color grading and sound design.


________________


SECTION 1: CLASSIFICATION & IDENTIFICATION
1.1 Official Classification

	Detail

	Location
	Paris / Milan / London / New York / Los Angeles / Shanghai / Tokyo
	1.2 Professional Context
The Post-Production Manager operates at a critical juncture in the commercial fashion production value chain, serving as the vital link between on-set production, creative vision, and final commercial delivery. According to industry descriptions, the role encompasses several key dimensions:


* Strategic Planning: Applying the post-production strategy defined by management and translating it into operational objectives
* Operational Execution: Overseeing the entire post-production process from raw asset ingestion to final delivery
* Team Leadership: Managing and mentoring post-production teams, often across multiple projects and locations
* Financial Management: Managing post-production budgets, negotiating vendor costs, and ensuring margin targets are met
* Quality Assurance: Maintaining the highest quality standards and ensuring brand consistency
* Vendor Management: Managing relationships with external post-production studios and freelancers
* Continuous Improvement: Driving process optimization and efficiency improvements
* Technology Integration: Adopting and implementing new post-production technologies
* Workflow Management: Designing and optimizing post-production workflows


According to industry job descriptions, the Post-Production Manager oversees "the end-to-end execution of the post-production cycle, ensuring timely delivery, exceptional quality, and adherence to budget targets" while playing "a pivotal role in driving operational excellence, vendor collaboration, and continuous process improvement."


The profession spans multiple specializations:


Specialization
	Focus
	Key Requirements

	Deep retouching knowledge, quality control expertise
	Video Editing Management

	Color Grading Management

SECTION 4: HR JOB DESCRIPTION
4.1 Position Overview
Position Title: Post-Production Manager (Responsable Post-Production)


Department: Post-Production / Production / Operations


Reports To: Executive Producer / Head of Production / Creative Director


Location: Paris, France (with potential international collaboration)


Employment Type: Full-time, Senior Management
4.2 Purpose of the Role
According to industry descriptions, the Post-Production Manager "organizes, optimizes, and supervises post-production processes, with the objective of delivering commercial content according to quality, cost, deadline, and brand imperatives." The role "applies the post-production strategy defined by management" and "can supervise a team or service and manage its budget."


In the commercial fashion context, the role oversees "the end-to-end execution of the post-production cycle, ensuring timely delivery, exceptional quality, and adherence to budget targets" while playing "a pivotal role in driving operational excellence, vendor collaboration, and continuous process improvement."

SECTION 17: CONCLUSION & NEXT STEPS
17.1 Summary
The Post-Production Manager role represents a strategic profession in the fashion industry, requiring a unique combination of technical expertise, creative understanding, and leadership skills. Success in this role demands:


1. Post-Production Planning: Ability to develop and execute post-production plans
2. Technical Excellence: Deep understanding of post-production software and workflows
3. Leadership: Managing and mentoring post-production teams
4. Financial Acumen: Understanding of costs, margins, and budget management
5. Vendor Management: Building and maintaining strong vendor relationships
6. Quality Management: Ensuring consistent quality standards
7. Process Improvement: Driving continuous improvement
8. Technology Integration: Adopting and implementing new technologies


According to industry research, the most successful post-production managers are those who combine technical expertise with strong leadership and financial acumen. The role is evolving with the adoption of digital technologies, including AI-powered post-production tools and automated quality control systems. The rise of sustainable production practices has also transformed the role, with post-production managers increasingly responsible for reducing waste and optimizing workflows.


The post-production industry is projected to continue growing, driven by increasing demand for commercial content across advertising and digital platforms. The future belongs to post-production managers who can combine operational excellence with strategic vision and technological innovation.

EOEX FASHION INDUSTRY JOB PROFILES COMPENDIUM
Document 58 of 60+: DIGITAL ASSET MANAGER (Responsable des Actifs Numériques / Digital Asset Manager / DAM Manager / Responsable de la Bibliothèque d'Images)


The Elegance of Excellence - Complete Career Framework


________________


EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the Digital Asset Manager role within the fashion industry, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to senior leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The Digital Asset Manager represents a critical and increasingly strategic role in the fashion industry, responsible for the organization, preservation, and distribution of a brand's digital assets, including images, videos, 3D assets, design files, and marketing content. According to industry definitions, a Digital Asset Manager "is responsible for the development, implementation, and maintenance of a digital asset management (DAM) system," ensuring that "digital content is consistent, accessible, secure, and usable across the organization." This role is distinguished by its position at the intersection of technology, creative production, and business operations, requiring a unique combination of technical expertise, organizational skills, and strategic thinking.


The profession has become increasingly important as fashion brands generate and distribute unprecedented volumes of digital content. According to industry research, the average fashion brand produces over 1,000 new assets per month, with this number growing exponentially with the rise of e-commerce, social media, and digital marketing. The Digital Asset Manager ensures that these assets are properly categorized, tagged, stored, and retrievable, maximizing their value and preventing costly duplication or loss. The role has evolved significantly with the adoption of AI-powered DAM systems, which can automatically tag, categorize, and suggest assets based on content recognition.


This document provides a detailed analysis of digital asset management requirements, comprehensive career pathways, value chain analysis, command chain mapping, key stakeholder identification, red flags, pain points, and vectors for long-term success. It also includes profiles for each major DAM sub-category, from fashion and retail DAM to media and entertainment DAM, with specific considerations for the unique requirements of fashion brands.


________________


SECTION 1: CLASSIFICATION & IDENTIFICATION
1.1 Official Classification
Attribute
	Detail
	French Job Title
	Responsable des Actifs Numériques / Digital Asset Manager / Responsable DAM / Responsable de la Bibliothèque d'Images / Archiviste Numérique
	English Job Title
	Digital Asset Manager / DAM Manager / Asset Librarian / Digital Content Manager / Media Asset Manager
	ROME Code
	M1602 - Gestion de portefeuille de clients; M1401 - Conduite d'enquêtes; E1201 - Photographie
	Convention Collective
	Presse Spécialisée Mode - IDCC 303; Audiovisuel - IDCC 3043; Photographie - IDCC 3060
	Job Family

	Supervises

	Location
	Paris / Milan / London / New York / Los Angeles / Tokyo / Shanghai
	1.2 Professional Context
The Digital Asset Manager operates at a critical juncture in the fashion digital ecosystem, serving as the guardian of the brand's digital memory and the facilitator of efficient content distribution. According to industry descriptions, the role encompasses several key dimensions:


* Asset Management Strategy: Defining and implementing DAM policies and workflows that ensure content is consistent, accessible, secure, and usable across the organization
* Metadata Management: Developing and implementing comprehensive metadata schemas that enable efficient search, discovery, and usage tracking
* Digital Preservation: Ensuring the long-term preservation of digital assets, including migration to new formats and systems as technology evolves
* Rights Management: Tracking usage rights, licenses, and permissions for digital assets, ensuring compliance with legal and contractual obligations
* Workflow Optimization: Designing and implementing efficient asset creation, review, approval, and distribution workflows
* System Administration: Managing DAM systems, including user access, system configuration, and integration with other enterprise systems
* User Training: Training creative, marketing, and production teams on DAM system usage and best practices
* Analytics and Reporting: Tracking asset usage, identifying inefficiencies, and recommending improvements


The role is distinguished by its dual focus on technology and human needs. As one industry expert notes, "A Digital Asset Manager needs to be part archivist, part librarian, part technologist, and part diplomat. They must understand the technical aspects of asset management while also understanding the creative and business needs of the organization."


According to the DAM Foundation, the Digital Asset Manager is responsible for "maximizing the value of an organization's digital assets by ensuring they are discoverable, accessible, and usable," and for "developing and enforcing standards that ensure consistency and quality across all digital assets." This strategic role has become increasingly important as fashion brands recognize that their digital assets represent a significant portion of their brand equity.


The profession spans multiple specializations:


Specialization
	Focus
	Key Requirements
	Creative DAM
	Managing creative assets for marketing and advertising
	Creative industry knowledge, metadata expertise
	Product DAM
	Managing product images and specifications for e-commerce
	Product data knowledge, e-commerce understanding
	Marketing DAM
	Managing marketing and campaign assets
	Marketing workflow knowledge, brand understanding
	Production DAM
	Managing production assets and technical files
	Production workflow knowledge, file format expertise
	Media DAM
	Managing video, audio, and multimedia assets
	Audiovisual knowledge, large file management
	Fashion DAM
	Managing fashion-specific assets (lookbooks, campaigns)
	Fashion industry knowledge, seasonal workflow understanding
	Enterprise DAM
	Managing assets across the entire organization
	Enterprise systems knowledge, change management

SECTION 4: HR JOB DESCRIPTION
4.1 Position Overview
Position Title: Digital Asset Manager (Responsable des Actifs Numériques)


Department: Digital / Operations / Marketing / Technology


Reports To: Head of Digital / CTO / Marketing Director / Production Director


Location: Paris, France


Employment Type: Full-time
4.2 Purpose of the Role
To develop, implement, and maintain the digital asset management system and workflows that ensure all digital content is consistent, accessible, secure, and usable across the organization. According to the DAM Foundation, the Digital Asset Manager "maximizes the value of an organization's digital assets by ensuring they are discoverable, accessible, and usable," and "develops and enforces standards that ensure consistency and quality across all digital assets."


In the fashion context, the role oversees "the entire lifecycle of digital assets, from creation to distribution and archive," ensuring that "marketing, creative, and production teams have access to the assets they need when they need them."

SECTION 17: CONCLUSION & NEXT STEPS
17.1 Summary
The Digital Asset Manager role represents a strategic profession in the fashion industry, requiring a unique combination of technical expertise, organizational skills, and business acumen. Success in this role demands:


1. Technical Expertise: Understanding of DAM platforms and technologies
2. Metadata Knowledge: Expertise in metadata schema design
3. Organizational Skills: Ability to organize large volumes of assets
4. Communication: Clear, effective communication with diverse stakeholders
5. Change Management: Ability to drive user adoption
6. Strategic Thinking: Understanding business objectives
7. Problem-Solving: Ability to identify and resolve challenges
8. Adaptability: Flexibility to changing technology


According to industry research, the most successful digital asset managers are those who combine technical expertise with strong business acumen and change management skills. The role is evolving with the adoption of AI-powered DAM systems, which can automatically tag, categorize, and suggest assets based on content recognition. The rise of cloud-based DAM solutions has also transformed the role, enabling global teams to access and manage assets from anywhere.


The DAM profession is projected to continue growing, driven by increasing volumes of digital content and the recognition that digital assets represent a significant portion of brand equity. The future belongs to DAM professionals who can combine technical excellence with strategic vision and change management expertise.

EOEX FASHION INDUSTRY JOB PROFILES COMPENDIUM
Document 59 of 60+: E-COMMERCE MANAGER (Responsable E-commerce / Directeur du Commerce Électronique / E-commerce Director)


The Elegance of Excellence - Complete Career Framework


________________


EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the E-commerce Manager role within the fashion industry, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to senior leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The E-commerce Manager represents a strategic and operational leadership role at the heart of the digital retail revolution, responsible for driving online sales, managing the e-commerce platform, and delivering an exceptional customer experience across all digital touchpoints. According to industry definitions, the E-commerce Manager "is responsible for the planning, development, and execution of an organization's e-commerce strategy," overseeing "the entire online sales process, from website development and user experience to digital marketing, customer service, and logistics." This role is distinguished by its position at the intersection of retail, technology, marketing, and customer experience, requiring a unique combination of commercial acumen, technical expertise, and strategic thinking.


The profession has become increasingly critical as fashion retail continues its digital transformation. According to industry research, global e-commerce sales are projected to reach $8.1 trillion by 2026, with fashion representing one of the largest and fastest-growing categories. The average fashion brand now generates 30-50% of its revenue through e-commerce channels, with this figure continuing to grow. The E-commerce Manager ensures that the brand's online presence is optimized to capture this growth, delivering a seamless, engaging, and profitable shopping experience.


The role has evolved significantly with the rise of social commerce, mobile shopping, AI-powered personalization, and omnichannel retail. According to industry experts, "the E-commerce Manager of 2026 must be equal parts retailer, technologist, marketer, and customer experience expert." This requires a unique combination of commercial acumen, technical understanding, and customer-centric thinking.


This document provides a detailed analysis of e-commerce management requirements, comprehensive career pathways, value chain analysis, command chain mapping, key stakeholder identification, red flags, pain points, and vectors for long-term success. It also includes profiles for each major e-commerce sub-category, from fashion and luxury e-commerce to DTC and marketplace management, with specific considerations for the unique requirements of fashion brands.


________________

	Location
	Paris / Milan / London / New York / Los Angeles / Tokyo / Shanghai
	1.2 Professional Context
The E-commerce Manager operates at a critical juncture in the fashion retail ecosystem, serving as the strategic and operational leader of the brand's online presence. According to industry descriptions, the role encompasses several key dimensions:


* Commercial Strategy: Developing and executing e-commerce strategies that drive sales, profitability, and customer acquisition
* Platform Management: Overseeing the e-commerce platform, ensuring optimal performance, user experience, and functionality
* Merchandising: Managing online product presentation, categories, and content to maximize conversion
* Digital Marketing: Coordinating digital marketing efforts including SEO, SEM, email, social, and paid media
* Customer Experience: Ensuring a seamless, engaging, and satisfying online shopping experience
* Analytics & Optimization: Using data to drive continuous improvement in conversion, average order value, and customer lifetime value
* Operations: Coordinating with logistics, fulfillment, and customer service to ensure operational excellence
* Innovation: Identifying and implementing new technologies and approaches to enhance the e-commerce offering


The role is distinguished by its dual focus on commercial outcomes and customer experience. As one industry expert notes, "The E-commerce Manager must be obsessed with both the numbers and the customer. They need to understand what drives sales while also understanding what creates loyal, repeat customers."


According to leading fashion brands, the E-commerce Manager is responsible for "delivering the brand's online vision, driving revenue growth, and ensuring the website is the best possible showcase for the brand." This requires a deep understanding of both retail fundamentals and digital capabilities.


The profession spans multiple specializations:


Specialization
	Focus
	Key Requirements

SECTION 4: HR JOB DESCRIPTION
4.1 Position Overview
Position Title: E-commerce Manager (Responsable E-commerce)


Department: E-commerce / Digital / Retail / Marketing


Reports To: Head of E-commerce / Chief Digital Officer / Marketing Director / CEO


Location: Paris, France


Employment Type: Full-time
4.2 Purpose of the Role
To develop, implement, and manage the e-commerce strategy that drives online sales, customer acquisition, and brand growth. According to industry descriptions, the E-commerce Manager "is responsible for the planning, development, and execution of an organization's e-commerce strategy," overseeing "the entire online sales process, from website development and user experience to digital marketing, customer service, and logistics."


In the fashion context, the role oversees "the end-to-end online retail experience, ensuring the website is the best possible showcase for the brand while driving revenue growth and customer loyalty."

According to industry research, a 1% improvement in conversion rate can increase revenue by 5-10%. Every percentage point counts."

SECTION 17: CONCLUSION & NEXT STEPS
17.1 Summary
The E-commerce Manager role represents a strategic profession in the fashion industry, requiring a unique combination of commercial acumen, digital expertise, and leadership skills. Success in this role demands:


1. Commercial Acumen: Understanding of retail economics and business drivers
2. Digital Expertise: Understanding of e-commerce platforms and technologies
3. Strategic Thinking: Ability to develop and execute strategy
4. Analytical Skills: Data-driven decision making
5. Customer Focus: Customer-centric thinking
6. Leadership: Team management and development
7. Adaptability: Flexibility in response to change
8. Innovation: New approaches and technologies


According to industry research, the most successful e-commerce managers are those who combine commercial acumen with digital expertise and customer-centric thinking. The role is evolving with the adoption of AI-powered personalization, social commerce, and omnichannel retail. The rise of mobile commerce has also transformed the role, with mobile now accounting for over 50% of e-commerce traffic.


The e-commerce profession is projected to continue growing, driven by increasing digital commerce penetration and the recognition that e-commerce is essential for brand growth. The future belongs to e-commerce managers who can combine commercial excellence with digital innovation and customer-centric leadership.

EOEX FASHION INDUSTRY JOB PROFILES COMPENDIUM
Document 60 of 60+: CHIEF DIGITAL OFFICER (CDO) (Directeur du Numérique / Chief Digital Officer / Directeur de la Transformation Digitale)


The Elegance of Excellence - Complete Career Framework


________________


EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This document provides a comprehensive career profile for the Chief Digital Officer (CDO) role within the fashion industry, structured according to the French "Référentiel des Métiers" framework. It encompasses the complete professional journey from educational requirements through to executive leadership, incorporating the Parisian Couture collective agreement classification criteria and the EOEX ecosystem value propositions.


The Chief Digital Officer represents the pinnacle of digital leadership in the fashion industry, responsible for defining and executing the digital vision and strategy of the entire organization. According to industry definitions, the CDO "is responsible for leading the digital transformation of an organization," overseeing "the development and implementation of digital strategies that drive business growth, customer engagement, and operational efficiency." This role sits at the intersection of technology, business strategy, and customer experience, requiring mastery of both digital capabilities and strategic business thinking.


The role has become increasingly critical as fashion brands navigate the digital revolution. According to industry research, 85% of fashion brands now have a CDO or equivalent digital leadership role, up from just 30% a decade ago. The CDO is responsible for ensuring that the brand remains competitive in an increasingly digital marketplace, leveraging technology to drive growth, enhance customer experience, and optimize operations. The role demands a unique combination of strategic vision, technological understanding, and business acumen.


The profession has evolved significantly with the rise of AI, social commerce, omnichannel retail, and digital-first business models. According to industry experts, "the CDO of 2026 must be equal parts visionary, technologist, and business leader. They need to understand where the industry is going, how technology can enable that future, and how to drive organizational change to get there."


This document provides a detailed analysis of CDO requirements, comprehensive career pathways, value chain analysis, command chain mapping, key stakeholder identification, red flags, pain points, and vectors for long-term success. It also includes profiles for each major CDO sub-category, from fashion and luxury digital leadership to retail and DTC transformation leadership, with specific considerations for the unique requirements of fashion brands.


________________


SECTION 1: CLASSIFICATION & IDENTIFICATION
1.1 Official Classification
Attribute
	Detail
	French Job Title
	Directeur du Numérique / Chief Digital Officer / Directeur de la Transformation Digitale / Directeur Digital
	English Job Title
	Chief Digital Officer / CDO / Head of Digital / Digital Transformation Director
	ROME Code

	Convention Collective

	Supervises

	Location
	Paris / Milan / London / New York / Los Angeles / Tokyo / Shanghai
	1.2 Professional Context
The Chief Digital Officer operates at the highest level of the fashion organization, serving as the strategic leader of the brand's digital transformation and ongoing digital strategy. According to industry descriptions, the role encompasses several key dimensions:


* Digital Vision & Strategy: Defining and articulating the brand's digital vision and strategic direction, ensuring alignment with overall business objectives
* Digital Transformation: Leading the organization's digital transformation, including process optimization, technology adoption, and cultural change
* Customer Experience: Ensuring exceptional digital customer experiences across all touchpoints
* Technology Leadership: Overseeing the technology strategy and architecture, ensuring it enables business growth and innovation
* Data & Analytics: Leveraging data to drive decision-making, personalization, and business insights
* Digital Marketing: Overseeing digital marketing strategy including e-commerce, social, and paid media
* Innovation: Identifying and implementing new technologies and business models
* Change Management: Driving organizational change and digital culture
* Executive Leadership: Serving as a member of the executive team, contributing to overall business strategy
* Stakeholder Management: Managing relationships with board members, investors, and key partners


The role is distinguished by its strategic and transformational nature. As one industry expert notes, "The CDO is not just the head of digital—they are the leader of digital transformation across the entire organization. They need to change how the company thinks, works, and competes in a digital world."


According to leading fashion brands, the CDO is responsible for "defining and executing the digital strategy that will drive the brand's growth and relevance in an increasingly digital marketplace." This requires a deep understanding of both the fashion industry and digital capabilities.


The profession spans multiple specializations:


Specialization
	Focus
	Key Requirements
	Fashion Digital Leadership
	Fashion brand digital strategy
	Fashion industry knowledge, strategic vision
	Luxury Digital Leadership
	Luxury brand digital strategy
	Luxury understanding, brand stewardship

	Retail expertise, omnichannel strategy
	DTC Digital Leadership
	Direct-to-consumer digital strategy
	DTC expertise, brand building
	E-commerce Leadership

	Change management expertise, leadership

SECTION 4: HR JOB DESCRIPTION
4.1 Position Overview
Position Title: Chief Digital Officer (Directeur du Numérique)


Department: Executive / Digital / Strategy


Reports To: CEO / President / Board of Directors


Location: Paris, France (with international travel)


Employment Type: Full-time, Executive
4.2 Purpose of the Role
To define and execute the digital vision and strategy that will drive the brand's growth, customer engagement, and competitive advantage in an increasingly digital marketplace. According to industry descriptions, the CDO "is responsible for leading the digital transformation of an organization," overseeing "the development and implementation of digital strategies that drive business growth, customer engagement, and operational efficiency."


In the fashion context, the role is responsible for "ensuring the brand remains relevant and competitive in a digital-first world, leveraging technology to enhance every aspect of the business, from customer experience to operations to innovation."

SECTION 10: ON-THE-JOB PAIN POINTS & CHALLENGES
10.1 Top 10 Pain Points
#
	Pain Point
	Description
	Impact Level

SECTION 17: CONCLUSION & NEXT STEPS
17.1 Summary
The Chief Digital Officer role represents the pinnacle of digital leadership in the fashion industry, requiring a unique combination of strategic vision, business acumen, and leadership capability. Success in this role demands:


1. Strategic Vision: Ability to define and execute digital strategy
2. Leadership: Ability to inspire and lead organizations
3. Business Acumen: Deep understanding of business drivers
4. Change Management: Ability to drive organizational transformation
5. Customer Focus: Customer-centric thinking
6. Technology Understanding: Understanding of technology capabilities
7. Communication: Clear, compelling communication
8. Resilience: Ability to handle pressure and setbacks


According to industry research, the most successful CDOs are those who combine strategic vision with strong execution capabilities and the ability to drive organizational change. The role is evolving with the rise of AI, social commerce, and digital-first business models. The CDO must ensure that the brand remains competitive in an increasingly digital marketplace.


The CDO profession is projected to continue growing in importance, driven by increasing digital commerce penetration and the recognition that digital transformation is essential for brand survival. The future belongs to CDOs who can combine strategic vision with execution excellence and organizational leadership.

	Key Insights Summary
1. Career Clarity: The compendium provides clear career pathways for over 60 distinct fashion roles, from entry-level to executive leadership.


2. French Context: Each profile incorporates French Référentiel des Métiers framework, ROME codes, and Parisian Couture collective agreement (IDCC 303) classification.


3. Industry Challenges: Common pain points identified across profiles include:


   * Late/no payment (14% paid on time)
   * Pressure to work for free (79%)
   * Toxic workplace behaviors (83%)
   * Income instability
   * Job insecurity (10% feel secure)


4. EOEX Value Proposition: Each profile demonstrates how EOEX ecosystem solutions address specific industry challenges through:


   * Akibai: Education and training
   * Ariella: Talent development
   * EOEX Studio: Production and creative services
   * EOEX Consulting: Strategic advisory
   * EOEX Publishing: Thought leadership
   * Cartésienes: Talent access and representation
   * EOEX Community: Networking and events


5. Career Progression: The compendium provides clear progression pathways from entry-level to executive leadership with income ranges for each level.


6. Competency Frameworks: Each profile includes comprehensive competency frameworks covering technical, creative, and behavioral competencies.


7. Legal Compliance: Each profile addresses French Labour Code requirements and industry-specific compliance considerations.
Recommendations for Use
The EOEX Fashion Industry Job Profiles Compendium can be utilized for:


1. Career Guidance: Individuals exploring fashion industry careers can understand requirements, pathways, and earning potential.


2. HR & Recruitment: Organizations can use profiles for job descriptions, candidate evaluation, and performance management.


3. Education & Training: Educational institutions can align curricula with industry requirements.


4. Professional Development: Professionals can identify skills gaps and development opportunities.


5. EOEX Strategy: The compendium validates EOEX ecosystem value propositions across all fashion industry roles.
